=== SOURCE CODE (java) ===
package org.example;

public class NumberConverter {

    public static String decimalToBinary(int decimalNum) {
        return Integer.toBinaryString(decimalNum);
    }

    public static int binaryToDecimal(String binaryNum) {
        return Integer.parseInt(binaryNum, 2);
    }

    public static String decimalToOctal(int decimalNum) {
        return Integer.toOctalString(decimalNum);
    }

    public static int octalToDecimal(String octalNum) {
        return Integer.parseInt(octalNum, 8);
    }

    public static String decimalToHex(int decimalNum) {
        return Integer.toHexString(decimalNum);
    }

    public static int hexToDecimal(String hexNum) {
        return Integer.parseInt(hexNum, 16);
    }
}

=== PROMPT ===
You are an expert programmer. Translate the following java code to py.
- Keep behavior identical, including inputs/outputs, side effects, edge cases, and exceptions.
- Use idiomatic py only when it does not change behavior.

```java
package org.example;

public class NumberConverter {

    public static String decimalToBinary(int decimalNum) {
        return Integer.toBinaryString(decimalNum);
    }

    public static int binaryToDecimal(String binaryNum) {
        return Integer.parseInt(binaryNum, 2);
    }

    public static String decimalToOctal(int decimalNum) {
        return Integer.toOctalString(decimalNum);
    }

    public static int octalToDecimal(String octalNum) {
        return Integer.parseInt(octalNum, 8);
    }

    public static String decimalToHex(int decimalNum) {
        return Integer.toHexString(decimalNum);
    }

    public static int hexToDecimal(String hexNum) {
        return Integer.parseInt(hexNum, 16);
    }
}
```

Use a short stable mapping path that preserves behavior.

Keep the reasoning compact and single-pass:
- Identify only the pieces that must remain equivalent: signatures, I/O contract, stateful behavior, control flow, and key API semantics.
- Turn that mapping into target-language structure promptly.
- Use one short trap scan for the single most likely language-pair failure mode.
- If a mismatch appears, patch only the affected local section and recheck once.
- If no concrete mismatch appears, close instead of reopening the mapping repeatedly.

Output only the translated py code in a code block.

Let's design:

class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def decimalToBinary(decimalNum):
        if decimalNum >= 0:
            return bin(decimalNum)[2:]
        else:
            return bin((1 << 32) + decimalNum)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def binaryToDecimal(binaryNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(binaryNum, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToOctal(decimalNum):
        if decimalNum >= 0:
            return oct(decimalNum)[2:]
        else:
            return oct((1 << 32) + decimalNum)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def octalToDecimal(octalNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(octalNum, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToHex(decimalNum):
        if decimalNum >= 0:
            return hex(decimalNum)[2:]
        else:
            return hex((1 << 32) + decimalNum)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def hexToDecimal(hexNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(hexNum, 16)

    @staticmethod
    def _parseInt(s, radix):
        # Mimic Java's Integer.parseInt(s, radix)
        if s is None:
            raise ValueError("null")
        # Java doesn't allow surrounding whitespace
        if s != s.strip():
            raise ValueError("For input string: \"" + s + "\"")
        # Also, Java doesn't allow empty string
        if len(s) == 0:
            raise ValueError("For input string: \"" + s + "\"")
        # Python's int() with base can parse, but it accepts underscores? No.
        # It also accepts Unicode digits? Maybe. But we can rely on int() to validate digits.
        # However, int() with base 2 accepts '0b'? No.
        # We'll use int() and catch ValueError to re-raise with Java-like message.
        try:
            value = int(s, radix)
        except ValueError:
            raise ValueError("For input string: \"" + s + "\"")
        # Range check for signed 32-bit int
        if value > 2147483647 or value < -2147483648:
            raise ValueError("For input string: \"" + s + "\"")
        return value

Now, let's implement `_parseInt` with manual validation. We can use a helper to check digits.

Pseudo:

    @staticmethod
    def _parseInt(s, radix):
        if s is None:
            raise ValueError("null")
        if s != s.strip():
            raise ValueError('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if len(s) == 0:
            raise ValueError('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        sign = 1
        index = 0
        if s[0] == '-':
            sign = -1
            index = 1
        elif s[0] == '+':
            index = 1
        if index >= len(s):
            raise ValueError('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        # define valid digits
        if radix == 2:
            valid = '01'
        elif radix == 8:
            valid = '01234567'
        elif radix == 16:
            valid = '0123456789abcdefABCDEF'
        else:
            raise ValueError("Invalid radix")  # not needed
        for ch in s[index:]:
            if ch not in valid:
                raise ValueError('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        # compute value
        value = int(s[index:], radix)  # s[index:] has no sign, but int() can parse it
        if sign == -1:
            value = -value
        # range check
        if value > 2147483647 or value < -2147483648:
            raise ValueError('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        return value

Now, let's think about the `NumberFormatException` class. If we define it, we need to place it before the class. The final code will be:

class NumberFormatException(ValueError):
    pass

class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def decimalToBinary(decimalNum):
        if decimalNum >= 0:
            return bin(decimalNum)[2:]
        return bin((1 << 32) + decimalNum)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def binaryToDecimal(binaryNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(binaryNum, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToOctal(decimalNum):
        if decimalNum >= 0:
            return oct(decimalNum)[2:]
        return oct((1 << 32) + decimalNum)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def octalToDecimal(octalNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(octalNum, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToHex(decimalNum):
        if decimalNum >= 0:
            return hex(decimalNum)[2:]
        return hex((1 << 32) + decimalNum)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def hexToDecimal(hexNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(hexNum, 16)

    @staticmethod
    def _parseInt(s, radix):
        if s is None:
            raise NumberFormatException("null")
        if s != s.strip():
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if len(s) == 0:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        sign = 1
        index = 0
        if s[0] == '-':
            sign = -1
            index = 1
        elif s[0] == '+':
            index = 1
        if index >= len(s):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        digits = s[index:]
        # Reject Python int() prefixes that Java would not accept.
        if radix == 16 and (digits.startswith('0x') or digits.startswith('0X')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if radix == 2 and (digits.startswith('0b') or digits.startswith('0B')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if radix == 8 and (digits.startswith('0o') or digits.startswith('0O')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        try:
            value = int(digits, radix)
        except ValueError:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if sign == -1:
            value = -value
        if value > 2147483647 or value < -2147483648:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        return value

But wait: In `_parseInt`, after we compute `value = int(digits, radix)`, we apply sign. But `int(digits, radix)` for a string like "0001" returns 1. For a string like "0" returns 0. For a string like "ffffffff" returns 4294967295. Then sign applied. Good.

However, there is a subtle issue: If `digits` is empty? We already check `index >= len(s)`, so if there is a sign and no digits, we raise. If no sign and empty string, `len(s)==0` raises. So digits is non-empty. Good.

Now, what about a string like "+" (only sign)? index=1, len(s)=1, index>=len(s) -> raise. Good.

Now, what about a string like "-" (only sign)? raise.

Now, what about a string like "+0"? index=1, digits="0", int=0, sign=1, value=0, valid. Good.

Now, what about a string like "-0"? sign=-1, digits="0", int=0, value=0, valid. Good.

Now, what about a string like "+00"? digits="00", int=0, valid.

Now, what about a string like "-00"? value=0, valid.

Now, what about a string like "+0001"? digits="0001", int=1, valid.

Now, what about a string like "-0001"? value=-1, valid.

Now, let's consider if we should use `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` to handle negative numbers more robustly. For negative numbers, `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` gives the unsigned 32-bit value. Then we could convert that to binary/octal/hex. For positive numbers within range, `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` gives the same value. For out-of-range positive numbers, it would mask to 32-bit, which might be more Java-like if the input were truncated. But as discussed, not necessary. However, using `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` would simplify the code: we could do `value = decimalNum & 0xffffffff` and then `bin(value)[2:]` for all inputs. But wait, for positive numbers, `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` gives the same value only if `decimalNum < 2^32`. If `decimalNum` is within Java int range (<= 2^31-1), it's fine. For negative numbers, it gives the unsigned representation. So we could write:

def decimalToBinary(decimalNum):
    return bin(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

But is this equivalent for all valid Java ints? Let's test:
- decimalNum = 0 -> 0 & 0xffffffff = 0 -> bin(0)[2:] = '0'. Good.
- decimalNum = 1 -> 1 -> '1'. Good.
- decimalNum = -1 -> -1 & 0xffffffff = 4294967295 -> '11111111111111111111111111111111'. Good.
- decimalNum = -2147483648 -> -2147483648 & 0xffffffff = 2147483648 -> '10000000000000000000000000000000'. Good.
- decimalNum = 2147483647 -> 2147483647 -> '1111111111111111111111111111111'. Good.

So using `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` works for all valid Java ints and also handles out-of-range by masking to 32 bits. Is that more faithful? It would mean that if someone passes 2147483648, it would mask to 0x80000000 (2147483648) and return '10000000000000000000000000000000'. But Java cannot receive 2147483648. If someone passes -2147483649, it would mask to 0x7fffffff (2147483647) and return '1111111111111111111111111111111'. That is a reasonable emulation of Java int overflow. But it's not specified. Using `(1 << 32) + decimalNum` for negative numbers only works for negative numbers, and for out-of-range negative numbers it gives a value that is not the 32-bit mask. For example, -2147483649 + 2^32 = 2147483647, which is the same as the mask? Actually -2147483649 & 0xffffffff = 2147483647? Let's compute: -2147483649 in two's complement 32-bit: -2147483649 + 2^32 = 2147483647. So it's the same. For -2147483650, -2147483650 + 2^32 = 2147483646, and -2147483650 & 0xffffffff = 2147483646. So for negative numbers, `(1 << 32) + decimalNum` is equivalent to `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` as long as `decimalNum` is in the range [-2^32+1, -1]? Actually for any negative `decimalNum`, `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` equals `decimalNum + 2^32` if `decimalNum >= -2^32`. If `decimalNum < -2^32`, then `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` equals `(decimalNum + 2^32) & 0xffffffff`, which is not simply `decimalNum + 2^32`. But for valid Java ints, `decimalNum >= -2^31`, so `decimalNum + 2^32` is between 2^31 and 2^32-1, and `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` equals that. So both are equivalent for valid Java ints. For out-of-range, `& 0xffffffff` is more consistent with 32-bit wrapping. Should we use `& 0xffffffff`? It's simpler and more robust. Let's consider: `bin(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]` for `decimalNum = -1` gives `bin(4294967295)[2:]` = '11111111111111111111111111111111'. Good. For `decimalNum = 0`, gives '0'. Good. For `decimalNum = 1`, gives '1'. Good. So we can use this for all three conversion methods. It also handles positive numbers up to 2^32-1, but Java ints are only up to 2^31-1. For positive numbers within Java int range, it's fine. For negative numbers, it's fine. So I'll use `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` to simplify.

But wait: For `decimalToOctal`, `oct(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]`. For `decimalNum = -1`, `decimalNum & 0xffffffff` = 4294967295, `oct(4294967295)[2:]` = '37777777777'. Good. For `decimalNum = 0`, '0'. Good.

For `decimalToHex`, `hex(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]`. For `decimalNum = -1`, 'ffffffff'. Good.

This is cleaner. Let's use it.

Now, what about `decimalToBinary` for `decimalNum = 2147483648` (out of range)? `2147483648 & 0xffffffff` = 2147483648, `bin(2147483648)[2:]` = '10000000000000000000000000000000'. If we had used `if decimalNum >= 0: bin(decimalNum)[2:]`, it would also return the same. So no difference for this value. For `decimalNum = 4294967295` (2^32-1), `4294967295 & 0xffffffff` = 4294967295, `bin(4294967295)[2:]` = '11111111111111111111111111111111'. If we used `if decimalNum >= 0`, it would also return the same. So for non-negative out-of-range up to 2^32-1, it's the same. For `decimalNum = 4294967296` (2^32), `4294967296 & 0xffffffff` = 0, `bin(0)[2:]` = '0'. If we used `if decimalNum >= 0`, it would return '100000000000000000000000000000000' (33 bits). Which is more Java-like? Java int cannot be 2^32. If we think of Java int overflow, 4294967296 would be truncated to 0. So `& 0xffffffff` is more Java-like. So using `& 0xffffffff` is better.

Thus, we can define:

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToBinary(decimalNum):
        return bin(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToOctal(decimalNum):
        return oct(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToHex(decimalNum):
        return hex(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

This is concise and preserves behavior for valid Java ints.

Now, let's think about the `_parseInt` method's range check. We use `value > 2147483647 or value < -2147483648`. But what about a string like "0xffffffff" in hex? int returns 4294967295, range check raises. Good.

Now, what about a string like "-0xffffffff" in hex? int returns -4294967295, range check raises. Good.

Now, what about a string like "0x80000000" in hex? int returns 2147483648, range check raises. Good.

Now, what about a string like "-0x80000000" in hex? int returns -2147483648, valid. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the `NumberFormatException` class being a subclass of `ValueError`. In Python, `except ValueError` will catch it. Good.

Now, let's think about the final code structure. We'll have:

class NumberFormatException(ValueError):
    pass

class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def decimalToBinary(decimalNum):
        return bin(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def binaryToDecimal(binaryNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(binaryNum, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToOctal(decimalNum):
        return oct(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def octalToDecimal(octalNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(octalNum, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToHex(decimalNum):
        return hex(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def hexToDecimal(hexNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(hexNum, 16)

    @staticmethod
    def _parseInt(s, radix):
        if s is None:
            raise NumberFormatException("null")
        if s != s.strip():
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if len(s) == 0:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        sign = 1
        index = 0
        if s[0] == '-':
            sign = -1
            index = 1
        elif s[0] == '+':
            index = 1
        if index >= len(s):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        digits = s[index:]
        if radix == 16 and (digits.startswith('0x') or digits.startswith('0X')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if radix == 2 and (digits.startswith('0b') or digits.startswith('0B')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if radix == 8 and (digits.startswith('0o') or digits.startswith('0O')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        try:
            value = int(digits, radix)
        except ValueError:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if sign == -1:
            value = -value
        if value > 2147483647 or value < -2147483648:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        return value

Now, let's test a few edge cases manually:

- `NumberConverter.decimalToBinary(-1)` -> `bin(-1 & 0xffffffff)[2:]` = `bin(4294967295)[2:]` = '11111111111111111111111111111111'. Good.
- `NumberConverter.binaryToDecimal("11111111111111111111111111111111")` -> `_parseInt("...", 2)`. s no whitespace, len>0, no sign, digits="...", not prefix, int=4294967295, sign=1, value=4294967295, range check raises NumberFormatException. Good.
- `NumberConverter.binaryToDecimal("-10000000000000000000000000000000")` -> int=-2147483648, range ok, return -2147483648. Good.
- `NumberConverter.hexToDecimal("0x10")` -> digits="0x10", radix=16, starts with "0x", raise NumberFormatException. Good.
- `NumberConverter.hexToDecimal("0b10")` -> digits="0b10", radix=16, starts with "0b" but radix != 2, no reject, int("0b10",16)=2832, range ok, return 2832. Java returns 2832. Good.
- `NumberConverter.octalToDecimal("0o17")` -> digits="0o17", radix=8, starts with "0o", raise. Good.
- `NumberConverter.binaryToDecimal("0b10")` -> digits="0b10", radix=2, starts with "0b", raise. Good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
class NumberFormatException(ValueError):
    pass


class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def decimalToBinary(decimalNum):
        return bin(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def binaryToDecimal(binaryNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(binaryNum, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToOctal(decimalNum):
        return oct(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def octalToDecimal(octalNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(octalNum, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToHex(decimalNum):
        return hex(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def hexToDecimal(hexNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(hexNum, 16)

    @staticmethod
    def _parseInt(s, radix):
        if s is None:
            raise NumberFormatException("null")
        if s != s.strip():
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if len(s) == 0:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        sign = 1
        index = 0
        if s[0] == '-':
            sign = -1
            index = 1
        elif s[0] == '+':
            index = 1

        if index >= len(s):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        digits = s[index:]

        # Reject Python int() prefixes that Java's parseInt does not accept.
        if radix == 16 and (digits.startswith('0x') or digits.startswith('0X')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if radix == 2 and (digits.startswith('0b') or digits.startswith('0B')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if radix == 8 and (digits.startswith('0o') or digits.startswith('0O')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        try:
            value = int(digits, radix)
        except ValueError:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        if sign == -1:
            value = -value

        if value > 2147483647 or value < -2147483648:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        return value
```

=== TRANSLATED CODE (py) ===
class NumberFormatException(ValueError):
    pass


class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def decimalToBinary(decimalNum):
        return bin(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def binaryToDecimal(binaryNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(binaryNum, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToOctal(decimalNum):
        return oct(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def octalToDecimal(octalNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(octalNum, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimalToHex(decimalNum):
        return hex(decimalNum & 0xffffffff)[2:]

    @staticmethod
    def hexToDecimal(hexNum):
        return NumberConverter._parseInt(hexNum, 16)

    @staticmethod
    def _parseInt(s, radix):
        if s is None:
            raise NumberFormatException("null")
        if s != s.strip():
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if len(s) == 0:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        sign = 1
        index = 0
        if s[0] == '-':
            sign = -1
            index = 1
        elif s[0] == '+':
            index = 1

        if index >= len(s):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        digits = s[index:]

        # Reject Python int() prefixes that Java's parseInt does not accept.
        if radix == 16 and (digits.startswith('0x') or digits.startswith('0X')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if radix == 2 and (digits.startswith('0b') or digits.startswith('0B')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')
        if radix == 8 and (digits.startswith('0o') or digits.startswith('0O')):
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        try:
            value = int(digits, radix)
        except ValueError:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        if sign == -1:
            value = -value

        if value > 2147483647 or value < -2147483648:
            raise NumberFormatException('For input string: "' + s + '"')

        return value
